CHAPTER 1




Introduction: Learning by
Comparison

   A molecule is a graph, a protein is a string, an image is a grid of pixels: almost nothing we want to learn
   from arrives as the tidy vectors our algorithms are written for. What every one of these objects does support is
   comparison. A chemist can judge whether two molecules are alike, and that judgment, made precise, turns out
   to be all a learning algorithm needs. Kernel methods rest on this bargain: hand an algorithm nothing but the
   table of pairwise similarities, the Gram matrix, and it will find the pattern without ever looking at the raw data.
   The bargain holds because a valid similarity is secretly an inner product, its norm a measure of smoothness,
   and the kernel-plus-algorithm pair a modular recipe: three views of one theorem, and the three threads this
   book follows to distributions, inference, calibrated uncertainty, deep networks, and working code. To learn is to
   compare; everything else is development.




1.1 The promise: patterns are relations

Suppose you are handed a thousand molecules and told which inhibit a target, or a stack of images to sort into
cats and dogs, or a genome to scan for genes. What these tasks share is not their raw data, which could hardly be
more different, but their shape: in each case you are looking for a pattern, a regularity that lets you say something
about an example you have not seen. Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini (2004) make this the organizing idea of the
whole field, pattern analysis, the search for stable relations in data. A classifier is a pattern that separates two
groups; a regression is a pattern that ties inputs to a number; a clustering is a pattern of proximity; a correlation is
a pattern shared between two views of the same objects.
The difficulty is that the data themselves rarely arrive as tidy vectors on which such patterns are visible. A molecule
is a graph, a protein is a string, an image is a grid of pixels whose interesting structure lives in no single coordinate.
The traditional response is feature engineering: hand-build a map from raw data into a vector space where the
pattern shows. Kernel methods make a quieter, more powerful bargain. They ask for one thing only.
22                                                                                        1 Introduction: Learning by Comparison



     The single ingredient


     A kernel is a function 𝑘(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) that scores how similar two examples 𝑥 and 𝑥′ are. Give a kernel method
     nothing but the table of pairwise scores on your data, the 𝑛 × 𝑛 Gram matrix 𝐾𝑖𝑗 = 𝑘(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ), and it will find
     the pattern. The examples may be vectors, strings, graphs, distributions, or anything at all; the algorithm
     never sees them, only their comparisons.


The Gram matrix is the interface between your data and every algorithm in this book. Below it is drawn for twelve
points on a line: slide the kernel’s bandwidth and watch the table of similarities reshape, the only channel through
which a kernel method perceives its world.



                                       Kij = k(xi , xj ), Gaussian kernel, bandwidth 1




Figure 1.1: Twelve points on a line and their Gram matrix 𝐾𝑖𝑗 = 𝑘(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) for a Gaussian kernel at bandwidth 1. A kernel is
positive definite exactly when this matrix is positive semidefinite for every sample; narrowing the bandwidth drives it toward
the identity.


A three-object calculation makes the bargain concrete. Give objects 𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶 the feature vectors Φ(𝐴) = (1, 0),
Φ(𝐵) = (0.8, 0.6), and Φ(𝐶) = (0, 1). Their Gram matrix is

                                                        1 0.8         0
                                                  𝐾 = (0.8 1         0.6) .
                                                        0 0.6         1

The entries say that 𝐵 resembles both endpoints while 𝐴 and 𝐶 are orthogonal; the kernel distances recover the
same geometry, for example 𝑑𝐾 (𝐴, 𝐵)2 = 1 + 1 − 2(0.8) = 0.4. The determinant is zero because the three vectors
live in a two-dimensional space, but 𝐾 is still PSD: singularity means redundant feature information, not an invalid
kernel. The failure mode is equally useful. If we changed 𝐾 𝐴𝐶 independently while keeping the other scores fixed,
the three pairwise judgments might cease to fit any common inner-product geometry. Kernel design is therefore
not assigning plausible similarities one pair at a time; it is making all comparisons compatible at once.
1.2 Three views of the same object                                                                                   23



1.2 Three views of the same object

Why should comparing examples be enough to learn from them? The three sources this book draws on answer the
question from three angles, and the answers are the same theorem seen from three sides. Understanding all three
is the fastest route to fluency, so the book is built to develop each in turn. These views are also what let the subject
outgrow its first home in classification. In the later parts the space itself comes under study, the objects being
compared become whole probability distributions, the questions become inferential and causal, and the answers
arrive with calibrated uncertainty and working code.


1.2.1 The geometric view: a kernel is an inner product

The first answer is that a valid kernel is never merely a similarity score; it is secretly a dot product in some space
of features. Whenever 𝑘 is positive definite, there is a map Φ sending each example into a (possibly infinite-
dimensional) inner-product space, its feature space, such that


                                               𝑘(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩.

Comparing with 𝑘 is thus doing geometry, measuring angles and lengths, in a space you never have to build.
Distances, projections, and separating planes all become available through the kernel alone. Lifting the data this
way is also what makes a separating plane likely to exist at all: Cover (1965) showed that patterns cast nonlinearly
into a higher-dimensional space are far more probably linearly separable there than in their native coordinates,
which is exactly the room a rich feature space buys. This is the view of Part I, and its precise form, the reproducing
kernel Hilbert space, is where the book begins.


1.2.2 The regularization view: the norm is smoothness

The second answer, developed at length by Schölkopf and Smola (2002), is that the feature space is not just any
inner-product space but a space of functions, and its norm measures how wildly a function wiggles. Learning is
then a tug of war written as

                                               1 𝑛
                                         min                         ‖𝑓‖2 ,
                                                 ∑ ℓ(𝑦𝑖 , 𝑓(𝑥𝑖 )) + 𝜆⏟
                                          𝑓    𝑛
                                               ⏟⏟  ⏟⏟⏟⏟⏟
                                                 𝑖=1                 stay smooth
                                                    fit the data

fit the training data, but pay for roughness. The kernel decides what “rough” means, and the penalty is what
keeps the fit from memorizing noise. This regularized-risk template is the engine of Part II, and the reason kernel
methods generalize rather than merely interpolate.


1.2.3 The modular view: kernel plus algorithm

The third answer, the pedagogical heart of Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini, is a clean separation of concerns. A
kernel method has two independent halves: a kernel that encodes what the data are and how they compare, and
an algorithm that consumes a Gram matrix and returns a pattern. Because the algorithm only ever touches the
matrix, the two halves mix and match freely.

   The two-part recipe


   Any pattern-analysis task decomposes as a kernel (choose or design it for your data type) plus an algorithm
   (ridge regression, an SVM, PCA, a two-sample test,...). Swap the kernel and the same algorithm now works
24                                                                                  1 Introduction: Learning by Comparison




     on strings or graphs; swap the algorithm and the same kernel now clusters instead of classifies. This
     modularity is why one small theory covers so much ground, and why the book can treat algorithms (Parts
     II, III, and V) separately from the kernels that feed them (Part VI).



1.3 The five debts of a learned predictor

A compact objective can conceal five different ways to be wrong. Bach organizes learning theory around ap-
proximation, estimation, and optimization error (Bach 2024, chaps. 2, 4, and 7). Kernel systems need two more
entries because the Gram matrix is a numerical object and because training assumptions may stop holding after
deployment.

Debt                                                        The question it forces us to answer
Approximation                                               Can the chosen RKHS express the target at the
                                                            required accuracy?
Estimation                                                  What is lost when a population risk or operator is
                                                            replaced by a finite sample?
Optimization                                                How far is the computed iterate from the empirical
                                                            optimizer?
Numerical approximation                                     What did finite precision, conditioning, random
                                                            features, sketches, or early stopping change?
Deployment                                                  Which sampling, exchangeability, stationarity, or
                                                            model assumptions can fail after training?


These debts cannot cancel one another. A solver residual says nothing about approximation error; universality says
nothing about a finite-sample rate; a close random-feature Gram matrix need not preserve calibrated uncertainty;
and a valid training guarantee does not survive an arbitrary shift in the input law. Every substantial method in
this book will be read through this ledger: identify the debt it controls, the currency of the guarantee, and the
debts it leaves unpaid.



1.4 The one obstacle: the solve

There is a price for working through the Gram matrix, and naming it early explains the shape of the later chapters.
The classical way to fit a kernel machine is to solve a linear system, or a quadratic program, over the whole 𝑛 × 𝑛
matrix at once. That matrix costs 𝑛2 memory to store and its solve costs on the order of 𝑛3 time, and neither
exponent has an 𝑛 you can escape, because the method is defined over all pairs of examples together. For a few
thousand examples this is nothing; for a few million it is a wall. Much of the modern subject, and all of Part III, is
about walking through that wall while keeping the kernel, whether by exploiting matrix structure, approximating
the Gram matrix, or replacing it by randomized features.



1.5 How to read this book

The twelve numbered parts follow one arc: establish the language, fit with a fixed kernel, learn to compute and
justify the fit, design geometry for new objects, move from points to distributions and operators, and end with
learned representations and accountable practice. Part 0 is a reference to lean on, not a hurdle to clear. The order
below is the intended first reading; the dependency map supports targeted research routes.
1.6 Common mistakes and practical implications                                                                     25



    • Part 0, Preliminaries. A self-contained reference for the mathematics the book leans on, from linear
      algebra and Hilbert spaces through probability, optimization, and functional analysis. Read it front to back
      as a refresher, or dip in when a later chapter cites a result by name.
    • Part I, The Language of Kernels. Positive-definite kernels, RKHS geometry, and the representer theorem.
    • Part II, Learning with a Fixed Kernel. KRR and the SVM family as one regularized-risk recipe under
      different losses and constraints.
    • Part III, Optimization and Scaling. Exact QP methods, online updates, matrix-free solvers, low-rank
      structure, random features, and sketches.
    • Part IV, Generalization, Approximation, and Limits. Complexity, spectra, inverse regularization,
      interpolation, universality, consistency, and modern interpolation theory.
    • Part V, Spectral Geometry and Unlabeled Structure. KPCA, clustering, multiview projections, MDS,
      and manifold regularization.
    • Part VI, Designing Kernels. Valid and computable similarities for vector, structured, spatial, geometric,
      operator-valued, and indefinite domains.
    • Part VII, Distributions as Objects. Mean embeddings, testing, transport, and quadrature.
    • Part VIII, Conditional, Stein, and Causal Inference. Regularized conditional operators, goodness of fit,
      identification, and learning from distributions.
    • Part IX, Gaussian Processes and Sequential Decisions. Model-based uncertainty, sparse inference,
      bandits, and Bayesian optimization.
    • Part X, Dynamics and Scientific Learning. Evolving systems, control, differential observations, inverse
      problems, and operator learning.
    • Part XI, Learning the Representation. MKL, infinite-width limits, deep kernels, variation spaces, and
      the research frontier.
    • Part XII, Reliable Practice. Shift, reproducible applications, accountability, and high-stakes scientific
      validation.
A note on the mathematics: the book assumes comfort with linear algebra and probability, states results precisely,
and gives the proofs the sources give, with the longest deferred to expandable blocks. Definitions, theorems, and
examples sit in numbered boxes that can be cited and linked from anywhere in the book, and the interactive
dependency map charts which earlier results each theorem leans on, so any proof can be traced back to its
foundations. Procedures get algorithm boxes; worked examples display only numbers that were actually computed,
each reproduced by a verification script committed alongside the text; and interactive figures, which run the real
kernel arithmetic live, appear where a picture teaches faster than a paragraph. Every chapter ends with pointers
into the literature, whose full details are in the bibliography. With the single idea in hand, comparison as the basis
of learning, we can begin.

   Further reading

   The three sources this book synthesizes are complementary points of entry. Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini
   (2004), Kernel Methods for Pattern Analysis, opens with the pattern-analysis framing and the modular
   kernel-plus-algorithm view used above. Schölkopf and Smola (2002), Learning with Kernels, develops the
   regularization and support-vector viewpoint in depth. The lecture course of Mairal and Vert, Machine
   Learning with Kernel Methods, gives the broadest modern survey, including the kernel families, distribution
   embeddings, and deep-learning connections of the later parts. The book itself now reaches well past both
   textbooks: Parts VIII through XIII draw on the post-2004 literature, from mean embeddings and Stein
   methods to Gaussian processes and the modern theory of interpolation, cited chapter by chapter where the
   results appear.



1.6 Common mistakes and practical implications

A symmetric similarity table is not automatically a kernel: validity is a statement about every finite quadratic
form, not about entries looking reasonable. The kernel is also not the algorithm; changing the loss, regularizer, or
26                                                                                           1 Introduction: Learning by Comparison



validation protocol changes the learned machine even when the Gram matrix stays fixed. Finally, a compelling
feature-space picture is an interpretation, not evidence of generalization. Keep data representation, kernel validity,
optimization, and statistical evaluation as four separate checks.



1.7 Summary and further reading

Kernel methods replace a representation problem with a comparison problem, but the comparison must be
geometrically coherent: its Gram matrices must behave like inner products. That one condition supports three
complementary readings used throughout the book: kernels define feature-space geometry, RKHS norms price
function variation, and Gram matrices let one algorithm accept many data types. The next chapter makes the
validity condition precise; (Schölkopf and Smola 2002) and (Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini 2004) develop the
geometric and pattern-analysis viewpoints in depth, while (Cover 1965) supplies the classical reason nonlinear
representations can simplify separation.



1.8 Exercises

     1. warm-up The chapter frames classification, regression, clustering, and correlation as four kinds of pattern.
        For each of the following, name which kind of pattern is being sought: sorting a stack of images into cats
        and dogs; predicting a molecule’s boiling point from its structure; grouping customers by how close their
        buying habits are; measuring how strongly the text of an article moves with the photograph beside it.
     2. warm-up A kernel method is told that it will only ever be shown the 𝑛 × 𝑛 Gram matrix 𝐾𝑖𝑗 = 𝑘(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ), never
        the examples 𝑥𝑖 themselves. Explain in one or two sentences why this is what lets the same algorithm run
        unchanged on vectors, strings, and graphs. Then state, in your own words, the one property the entries of 𝐾
        must carry so that the geometric view of Section 2 applies, that is, so that 𝑘(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ for some
        feature map Φ.

          Hint.
          The algorithm’s independence from the raw data is exactly the modular kernel-plus-algorithm split. The property
          you need on 𝐾 is the one the chapter names when it says a valid kernel is “secretly a dot product.”

     3. warm-up The chapter says the classical way to fit a kernel machine solves a linear system over the whole
        Gram matrix, at a cost of about 𝑛2 memory and 𝑛3 time, and that “neither exponent has an 𝑛 you can escape.”
        Roughly how many times more time does the solve take when the dataset grows from one thousand to one
        million examples? Explain why the modular view of Section 2 does not, on its own, remove this wall, and
        name which part of the book is devoted to walking through it.

          Hint.
          A factor of 103 more examples raises an 𝑛3 cost by (103 )3 . The wall lives in the algorithm’s use of the matrix, not
          in the choice of kernel, so swapping kernels leaves it untouched.

     4. exploration Open the interactive Gram-matrix figure for the twelve points on a line and narrow the kernel’s
        bandwidth toward zero while watching the table of similarities. Describe what the matrix approaches, and
        say which matrix it comes to resemble. Then argue, from that shape alone, why a kernel with a very small
        bandwidth “sees” each point as similar only to itself, and why an algorithm handed such a matrix has almost
        no relations left to learn from.

          Hint.
          Watch the off-diagonal entries as the bandwidth shrinks: a point stops resembling even its close neighbors. What
          is left is large only where 𝑖 = 𝑗. Compare the limiting table to the identity matrix.
                                                                                                                      27




CHAPTER 2




Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS

   Machine learning algorithms are usually written for vectors. Real data are strings, graphs, images, molecules,
   time series. Kernel methods resolve this mismatch with one idea: never touch the data directly, only compare
   pairs of points. But not any comparison will do; an arbitrary similarity score promises nothing. The condition
   that separates the useful comparisons from the rest is a single algebraic inequality, positive definiteness, and
   it buys far more than it appears to. Every kernel satisfying it is an inner product after an embedding into a
   Hilbert space, by Aronszajn’s theorem, and it carries a canonical space of functions, the reproducing kernel
   Hilbert space (RKHS), in which evaluating a function is an inner product and the norm measures how fast the
   function can vary. This chapter proves both facts, builds a toolbox for constructing and recognizing kernels,
   and works the theory out on the classical examples. Everything that follows in this book rests on these objects.




2.1 Why pairwise comparisons?

We want versatile algorithms: methods that process and analyze data without making any assumption on the
type of the data, whether vectors, strings, graphs or images. The approach developed here is to build methods on
pairwise comparisons. By imposing constraints on the pairwise comparison function, the positive definiteness
studied below, we will obtain a general framework for learning from data, namely optimization in a reproducing
kernel Hilbert space.
Concretely, we define a comparison function 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ on the data space 𝒳, and we represent a set of 𝑛
data points 𝒮 = {𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , … , 𝑥𝑛 } by the 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrix of all pairwise comparisons:


                                                [K]𝑖𝑗 ∶= 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ).

For instance, a set of three DNA-like strings such as 𝒮 = (aatcgagtcac, atggacgtct, tgcactact) becomes, once
a comparison function between strings is chosen, an ordinary 3 × 3 matrix of real numbers, and from that point
on the strings themselves never need to be looked at again.
Three remarks explain why this representation is so attractive, and what it costs.
   • Genericity. The matrix K is always an 𝑛 × 𝑛 matrix of real numbers, whatever the nature of the data. The
     same algorithm will therefore work for any type of data: vectors, strings, graphs, and so on.
28                                                                                              2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS



     • Modularity. There is total modularity between the choice of the comparison function 𝐾, which encodes
       what we know about the data, and the choice of the learning algorithm, which operates only on the matrix.
     • Scalability. The price is poor scalability with respect to the dataset size: the matrix takes 𝑛2 operations
       to compute and 𝑛2 memory to store. Chapter 12, Large-Scale Kernel Machines shows how to deal with
       large-scale problems; for now we accept the cost.

Not every comparison function will do. We now restrict ourselves to a particular class of pairwise comparison
functions, and this restriction is the source of all the structure to come.



2.2 Positive definite kernels

A useful comparison function should behave like a similarity measure, and the algebraic way to say “behaves like
an inner product” is positive definiteness.

     Definition (positive definite kernel)


     A positive definite (p.d.) kernel on a set 𝒳 is a function 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ that is symmetric,

                                             ∀(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ∈ 𝒳 2 ,   𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 𝐾(𝑥′ , 𝑥),
     and which satisfies, for all 𝑁 ∈ ℕ, all points (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , … , 𝑥𝑁 ) ∈ 𝒳 𝑁 and all coefficients (𝑎1 , 𝑎2 , … , 𝑎𝑁 ) ∈ ℝ𝑁 :
                                                    𝑁   𝑁
                                                    ∑ ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) ≥ 0.
                                                    𝑖=1 𝑗=1



     Remark (similarity matrices)


     Equivalently, a kernel 𝐾 is p.d. if and only if, for any 𝑁 ∈ ℕ and any set of points (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , … , 𝑥𝑁 ) ∈ 𝒳 𝑁 ,
     the similarity matrix [K]𝑖𝑗 ∶= 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) is symmetric positive semidefinite. Indeed, the double sum in the
     definition is exactly the quadratic form 𝑎⊤ K𝑎. Kernel methods are algorithms that take such matrices as
     input.


Note the slightly unfortunate but standard terminology: a positive definite kernel produces positive semidefinite
matrices; the quadratic form is allowed to vanish on nonzero coefficient vectors.

     Remark (terminology and history)


     The word kernel predates machine learning by decades. As Schölkopf and Smola (2002) recall, it entered
     mathematics through the theory of integral operators studied by Hilbert and others, where a function 𝐾 is
     the kernel of the operator

                                               (𝑇𝐾 𝑓)(𝑥) = ∫ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) 𝑓(𝑥′ ) 𝑑𝑥′ .

     The same object travels under many names across communities: reproducing kernel, Mercer kernel, ad-
     missible kernel, support vector kernel, nonnegative definite kernel, and, in statistics, covariance function.
     Schölkopf and Smola also caution that the name “positive definite kernel” sits slightly awry with matrix
     theory, where “definite” is often reserved for the strict case in which the quadratic form vanishes only for
     the zero coefficient vector. Our kernels are merely “semidefinite” in that stricter sense, which is exactly why
     their Gram matrices are p.s.d. rather than p.d.
2.2 Positive definite kernels                                                                                                                 29



What does the inequality actually demand? It is best read as a promise about squared lengths. If the kernel were
                                                                                                                     2
secretly an inner product, 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) = ⟨Φ(𝑥𝑖 ), Φ(𝑥𝑗 )⟩, then the double sum would telescope into ‖‖ ∑𝑖 𝑎𝑖 Φ(𝑥𝑖 )‖‖ ,
which can never be negative. Positive definiteness is exactly the algebraic shadow of that geometric fact: it is
the one condition, stated without ever naming a feature map Φ, that guarantees a Φ can be found. Aronszajn’s
theorem below turns the promise into a construction; for now, the condition is the entrance ticket.
Concretely, positive definiteness is something one can watch: choose a handful of points, form their Gram matrix,
and check whether that matrix is positive semidefinite. The next figure does this live for points on a line, and lets
you see how the choice of kernel and its bandwidth reshape the entries and the eigenvalues of the matrix.



                                             Kij = k(xi , xj ), Gaussian kernel, bandwidth 1




Figure 2.1: Twelve points on a line and their Gram matrix 𝐾𝑖𝑗 = 𝑘(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) for a Gaussian kernel at bandwidth 1. A kernel is
positive definite exactly when this matrix is positive semidefinite for every sample; narrowing the bandwidth drives it toward
the identity.



2.2.1 The simplest p.d. kernels

The definition is best absorbed through three foundational examples, in increasing generality.

    Lemma (product kernel on ℝ)


    Let 𝒳 = ℝ. The function 𝐾 ∶ ℝ2 → ℝ defined by

                                                   ∀(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ∈ ℝ2 ,      𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 𝑥𝑥′
    is p.d.
    Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
    is implied. Proof status. Proved immediately below.



Proof. Symmetry is clear: 𝑥𝑥′ = 𝑥′ 𝑥. For positivity, the double sum collapses into a square:

                                                    𝑖=1 𝑗=1                 𝑖=1

     Let 𝒳 = ℝ𝑑 . The function 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 2 → ℝ defined by

     is p.d. It is often called the linear kernel.
     Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
     is implied. Proof status. Proved immediately below.



Proof. Symmetry: ⟨𝑥, 𝑥′ ⟩ℝ𝑑 = ⟨𝑥′ , 𝑥⟩ℝ𝑑 . Positivity, by bilinearity of the inner product:

                                                𝑁   𝑁                            𝑁  2

                                              𝑖=1 𝑗=1                         𝑖=1            ℝ𝑑


                                                                                                                                                 

The same computation proves something far more ambitious. Nothing in the argument used the structure of 𝒳; it
only used that the compared objects live in an inner product space. So we may first move the data anywhere we
like.

     Lemma (kernels from embeddings)


     Let 𝒳 be any set, and Φ ∶ 𝒳 → ℝ𝑑 any mapping. Then the function 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 2 → ℝ defined as follows is p.d.:

                                            ∀(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ∈ 𝒳 2 ,    𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ 𝑑 .
                                                                                                    ℝ
     Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
     is implied. Proof status. Proved immediately below.



Proof. Symmetry: ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ℝ𝑑 = ⟨Φ(𝑥′ ), Φ(𝑥)⟩ℝ𝑑 . Positivity:

                                        𝑁   𝑁                                        𝑁2
                                       ∑ ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 ⟨Φ(𝑥𝑖 ), Φ(𝑥𝑗 )⟩ 𝑑 = ‖‖ ∑ 𝑎𝑖 Φ(𝑥𝑖 )‖‖                   ≥ 0.
                                                                       ℝ                           ℝ𝑑
                                       𝑖=1 𝑗=1                                    𝑖=1


                                                                                                                                                 

This lemma is the picture to keep in mind throughout: points 𝑥 in an arbitrary set 𝒳 are mapped by a feature
map Φ into a space where inner products make sense, and the kernel is the inner product of the images. The
remarkable fact, proved below, is that every p.d. kernel arises this way.
2.3 Kernels as inner products: Aronszajn’s theorem                                                                           31



2.2.2 A first nonlinear example: the polynomial kernel

Embeddings let us build kernels that are nonlinear in the original variables. For 𝑥 = (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 )⊤ ∈ ℝ2 , consider the
map into ℝ3 of degree-two monomials, Φ(𝑥) = (𝑥12 , √2 𝑥1 𝑥2 , 𝑥22 ). Then

                                                                                                       2
               𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ 3 = 𝑥12 𝑥1′2 + 2𝑥1 𝑥2 𝑥1′ 𝑥2′ + 𝑥22 𝑥2′2 = (𝑥1 𝑥1′ + 𝑥2 𝑥2′ ) = ⟨𝑥, 𝑥′ ⟩2ℝ2 .
                                              ℝ

The squared inner product is therefore p.d. on ℝ2 by the embedding lemma, and it lets linear machinery capture
quadratic structure: a function that is linear in Φ(𝑥) is a quadratic function of 𝑥. Geometrically, a curved boundary
in the plane, such as an ellipse, becomes a hyperplane in the three-dimensional feature space.
The feature map that realizes a kernel is far from unique. Schölkopf and Smola (2002) make this vivid for the
polynomial kernel by exhibiting two different maps that produce the same kernel. One lists the ordered products
of coordinates, [𝑥]𝑗1 [𝑥]𝑗2 ⋯ [𝑥]𝑗𝑑 , landing in a space of dimension 𝑁 𝑑 ; the other keeps only the distinct unordered
monomials and compensates for their multiplicities by weighting each with the square root of the number of
orderings that generate it. In the plane at degree two the second map is exactly the Φ(𝑥) = (𝑥12 , √2 𝑥1 𝑥2 , 𝑥22 ) above,
the √2 being the square root of the two orderings of the product 𝑥1 𝑥2 . Both maps compute ⟨𝑥, 𝑥′ ⟩2ℝ2 , so a single
kernel lives at once in feature spaces of different dimensions. What the kernel pins down is the geometry, not the
coordinates.

    Remark (why the √2?)


   Those square-root-of-multiplicity weights are not cosmetic. Schölkopf and Smola (2002) observe that they
   are precisely what makes the feature map commute with rotations: up to an overall scale, the homogeneous
   kernel ⟨𝑥, 𝑥′ ⟩𝑑 is the only kernel whose degree-𝑑 monomial feature map is invariant under orthogonal
   transformations of the input space. So the natural weighting of the monomials is forced by the demand
   that relabeling the coordinate axes, an operation that changes nothing about the data, must not change the
   geometry the kernel induces.


    Exercise


   Show that 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ⟨𝑥, 𝑥′ ⟩𝑑ℝ𝑝 is p.d. on 𝒳 = ℝ𝑝 for any 𝑑 ∈ ℕ. (A one-line proof will be available once
   we know that products of p.d. kernels are p.d.; a direct proof expands the power into monomial features,
   generalizing the computation above.)
    Verification artifact. checks/example-ch01-example-ch01-1.json records the example source hash and verification scope.




2.3 Kernels as inner products: Aronszajn’s theorem

The embedding lemma produced p.d. kernels from feature maps. The converse question is the deep one: given a
p.d. kernel, defined only by its algebraic properties, is it secretly an inner product after some embedding? The
answer is yes, always, provided we allow the target space to be an infinite-dimensional generalization of Euclidean
space.

    Theorem (Aronszajn, 1950)

    𝐾 is a p.d. kernel on the set 𝒳 if and only if there exists a Hilbert space ℋ and a mapping

                                                           Φ∶𝒳→ℋ
                               ′
    such that, for any 𝑥, 𝑥 in 𝒳:
32                                                                                                         2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS




                                                       𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ .
                                                                                      ℋ
     Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
     is implied. Proof status. No separate proof is attached to this result; independent technical review must determine whether the
     surrounding derivation is sufficient.



One direction is already done: the proof of the embedding lemma works verbatim with ℝ𝑑 replaced by any Hilbert
space ℋ, since it only uses bilinearity, symmetry and nonnegativity of the squared norm. The content of the
theorem is the other direction. Before proving it, we recall the definitions it involves.


2.3.1 In case of need: Hilbert spaces

Aronszajn’s theorem promises a Hilbert space, so the proof must know exactly what that phrase demands. Two
ingredients define it: an inner product, which supplies the geometry, and completeness, which keeps limits from
escaping the space.

     Definitions (inner product, Hilbert space)


     An inner product on an ℝ-vector space ℋ is a mapping (𝑓, 𝑔) ↦ ⟨𝑓, 𝑔⟩ℋ from ℋ 2 to ℝ that is bilinear,
     symmetric, and such that ⟨𝑓, 𝑓⟩ℋ > 0 for all 𝑓 ∈ ℋ ∖ {0}. A vector space endowed with an inner product is
     called pre-Hilbert. It carries the norm ‖𝑓‖ℋ = ⟨𝑓, 𝑓⟩1/2
                                                          ℋ . A Cauchy sequence (𝑓𝑛 )𝑛≥0 is a sequence whose
     elements become progressively arbitrarily close to each other:

                                                       lim      sup ‖𝑓𝑛 − 𝑓𝑚 ‖ℋ = 0.
                                                      𝑁→+∞ 𝑛,𝑚≥𝑁

     A Hilbert space is a pre-Hilbert space that is complete for the norm ‖⋅‖ℋ : every Cauchy sequence in ℋ
     converges in ℋ.


In one sentence: a Hilbert space is Euclidean geometry, with its lengths, angles and projections, that survives the
passage to infinitely many dimensions. The inner product supplies the geometry, and completeness is the safety
net.
Completeness is not a technical luxury. It is necessary to keep the good convergence properties of Euclidean
spaces in an infinite-dimensional context; without it, limits that “should” exist may fall outside the space, and
basic tools such as orthogonal projections and the Riesz representation theorem fail. The rational numbers are the
cautionary analogy: a sequence of rationals can march steadily toward √2 yet have no rational limit, and a space
of functions can misbehave in the same way unless we complete it, which is exactly the final step of the RKHS
construction below.


2.3.2 Proof of the converse: the finite case

When 𝒳 is finite the theorem is pure linear algebra, and the proof shows exactly where the feature map comes
from: the spectral decomposition of the kernel matrix.

Proof (finite case). Assume 𝒳 = {𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , … , 𝑥𝑁 } is finite of size 𝑁. Any p.d. kernel 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ is entirely
defined by the 𝑁 × 𝑁 symmetric positive semidefinite matrix [K]𝑖𝑗 ∶= 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ). This matrix can be diagonalized
on an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors (𝑢1 , 𝑢2 , … , 𝑢𝑁 ) with nonnegative eigenvalues 0 ≤ 𝜆1 ≤ … ≤ 𝜆𝑁 , that is,

                                                 𝑁                   𝑁
                               𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) = [ ∑ 𝜆𝑙 𝑢𝑙 𝑢⊤
                                                        𝑙 ] = ∑ 𝜆𝑙 [𝑢𝑙 ]𝑖 [𝑢𝑙 ]𝑗 = ⟨Φ(𝑥𝑖 ), Φ(𝑥𝑗 )⟩ 𝑁 ,
                                                              𝑖𝑗                                             ℝ
                                                𝑙=1                 𝑙=1
2.3 Kernels as inner products: Aronszajn’s theorem                                                                                            33



with

                                                                   √𝜆1 [𝑢1 ]𝑖
                                                        Φ(𝑥𝑖 ) = (    ⋮       ).
                                                                  √𝜆𝑁 [𝑢𝑁 ]𝑖

                                                                                                                                              

It is worth reading off what this proof actually did, because the idea recurs in a more abstract guise for infinite 𝒳.
The feature map is nothing but the square root of the kernel matrix, taken through its eigenbasis: the coordinates
of Φ(𝑥𝑖 ) are the entries of the 𝑖-th data point along each principal axis 𝑢𝑙 , rescaled by √𝜆𝑙 . Directions with large
eigenvalue stretch the embedding, directions with zero eigenvalue collapse it. The nonnegativity of the eigenvalues,
which is precisely positive semidefiniteness, is exactly what makes those square roots real, and hence what makes
the embedding possible at all.


2.3.3 The general case: a short history

For infinite 𝒳 the result was established in stages: Mercer (1909) proved it for 𝒳 = [𝑎, 𝑏] ⊂ ℝ, and more generally
for 𝒳 compact, with 𝐾 continuous; Kolmogorov (1941) for 𝒳 countable; and Aronszajn (1944, 1950) in the general
case. We will go through the proof of the general case by introducing the concept at the heart of the theory, the
reproducing kernel Hilbert space, which provides a canonical choice of ℋ valid without any restriction on 𝒳 or
on 𝐾.
Mercer’s special case deserves a closer look, because it produces the embedding by a route that is the exact
continuous analogue of the finite spectral construction: eigenvectors are replaced by eigenfunctions. Both
Schölkopf and Smola (2002) and Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini (2004) develop it, and it is the standard way to see a
kernel as a weighted sum of feature coordinates.

    Theorem (Mercer, 1909)

    Let 𝒳 be a compact subset of ℝ𝑛 and let 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ be continuous and symmetric, with a positive
    integral operator, that is,

                                   ∫       𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) 𝑓(𝑥) 𝑓(𝑥′ ) 𝑑𝑥 𝑑𝑥′ ≥ 0         for all 𝑓 ∈ 𝐿2 (𝒳).
                                     𝒳×𝒳

   Then 𝐾 admits a uniformly convergent expansion
                                                                     ∞
                                                      𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ∑ 𝜙𝑗 (𝑥) 𝜙𝑗 (𝑥′ ),
                                                                    𝑗=1

   where the feature coordinates are 𝜙𝑗 = √𝜇𝑗 𝜓𝑗 , built from the eigenvalues 𝜇𝑗 ≥ 0 and the eigenfunctions
   𝜓𝑗 of the integral operator. The eigenfunctions (𝜓𝑗 )𝑗≥1 are orthonormal in 𝐿2 (𝒳); the scaled coordinates
   (𝜙𝑗 )𝑗≥1 are therefore orthogonal, but not orthonormal, with squared norm ‖𝜙𝑗 ‖2𝐿2 = 𝜇𝑗 .
    Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
    is implied. Proof status. No separate proof is attached to this result; independent technical review must determine whether the
    surrounding derivation is sufficient.



The expansion is the promised embedding: the map 𝑥 ↦ (𝜙1 (𝑥), 𝜙2 (𝑥), … ) into the sequence space ℓ2 realizes 𝐾
as an inner product, exactly as the map 𝑥𝑖 ↦ (√𝜆𝑙 [𝑢𝑙 ]𝑖 )𝑙 did in the finite case, with the eigenfunctions 𝜓𝑗 now
playing the role of the principal axes 𝑢𝑙 and the eigenvalues 𝜇𝑗 the role of the 𝜆𝑙 . This is the Mercer route to the
feature space. Its price is the hypotheses it needs, compactness of 𝒳, continuity of 𝐾, and a reference measure; the
RKHS route developed below reaches the same conclusion while asking for none of them.
34                                                                                     2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS



2.4 Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces

2.4.1 Mapping data points to functions

Among the Hilbert spaces ℋ whose existence Aronszajn’s theorem asserts, one particular realization will be of
special interest to us. It is a space of functions from 𝒳 to ℝ: each data point 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 will be represented by a
function Φ(𝑥) = 𝐾𝑥 in ℋ.
To see what this could mean, consider 𝒳 = ℝ. We could decide to represent each scalar 𝑥 ∈ ℝ by a Gaussian
function centered at 𝑥:

                                                                1
                                                            −       (𝑥−𝑡)2
                                                𝐾𝑥 ∶ 𝑡 ↦ 𝑒 2𝛼2               .

Two points 𝑥 and 𝑦 then become two Gaussian bumps sitting at 𝑥 and 𝑦 on the real line, and their overlap
measures their similarity. The questions this raises are exactly the ones the RKHS construction answers: what is
the corresponding space ℋ, if it exists, and what is its inner product?
One point deserves emphasis before the formal definition. The data are mapped to Gaussian functions living in a
Hilbert space ℋ, but ℋ is much richer and contains much more than Gaussian bumps. It is in this larger space
that prediction functions 𝑓 will live, and they will interact with the embedded data through the inner product:
𝑓(𝑥) = ⟨𝑓, Φ(𝑥)⟩ℋ . This identity, evaluation as an inner product, is the defining property we now formalize.


2.4.2 Definition and the reproducing property

The bump picture becomes a definition once we ask two things of the kernel: that its sections 𝐾𝑥 belong to the
space, and that they mediate evaluation.

     Definition (reproducing kernel, RKHS)


     Let 𝒳 be a set and ℋ ⊂ ℝ𝒳 be a class of functions forming a (real) Hilbert space with inner product ⟨⋅, ⋅⟩ℋ .
     The function 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 2 → ℝ is called a reproducing kernel (r.k.) of ℋ if

        1. ℋ contains all functions of the form

                                                ∀𝑥 ∈ 𝒳,    𝐾𝑥 ∶ 𝑡 ↦ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑡);

        2. for every 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 and 𝑓 ∈ ℋ, the reproducing property holds:

                                                      𝑓(𝑥) = ⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ .

     If a r.k. exists, then ℋ is called a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS).


The second condition is the whole point, so it is worth saying in words. It asserts that evaluating a function 𝑓 at a
point 𝑥, an operation that looks nothing like an inner product, is in fact the inner product of 𝑓 against one special
function, the kernel section 𝐾𝑥 . Reading a function’s value at 𝑥 becomes measuring its overlap with the bump
sitting at 𝑥. The kernel therefore plays a double role: each 𝐾𝑥 is at once a member of the space ℋ (a function we
can evaluate elsewhere) and the probe that reports the value of any other member at 𝑥. That single identity is
what will let us turn nonlinear learning in 𝒳 into linear algebra in ℋ.
Why do we care? Because the RKHS gives us a simple recipe to do machine learning:

     • Map the data 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 to a high-dimensional Hilbert space ℋ, the RKHS, through the kernel mapping
       Φ ∶ 𝒳 → ℋ with Φ(𝑥) = 𝐾𝑥 .
2.4 Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces                                                                                                        35



    • In ℋ, consider only simple linear models 𝑓(𝑥) = ⟨𝑓, Φ(𝑥)⟩ℋ ; this is literally the reproducing property.
    • If 𝒳 = ℝ𝑝 , a function that is linear in Φ(𝑥) may be highly nonlinear in 𝑥, as the polynomial example already
      showed.
For instance, for supervised learning, given training data (𝑦𝑖 , 𝑥𝑖 )𝑖=1,…,𝑛 , we may want to minimize the regularized
empirical risk

                                                     1 𝑛
                                                 min   ∑ 𝐿(𝑦𝑖 , 𝑓(𝑥𝑖 )) + 𝜆‖𝑓‖2ℋ ,
                                                 𝑓∈ℋ 𝑛
                                                       𝑖=1

where 𝐿 is a loss function and 𝜆 ≥ 0 a regularization parameter. Making sense of this optimization problem, and
solving it, occupies the coming chapters; the smoothness interpretation of the penalty ‖𝑓‖2ℋ closes the present
one.


2.4.3 An equivalent definition: continuous evaluation

The definition of a r.k. looks tailored to the kernel 𝐾. In fact, being an RKHS is an intrinsic property of the Hilbert
space ℋ, independent of any particular kernel: it says that pointwise evaluation is a well-behaved operation.

   Theorem (RKHS = continuous evaluation functionals)


   The Hilbert space ℋ ⊂ ℝ𝒳 is a RKHS if and only if for any 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳, the linear mapping

                                                    𝐹 ∶ ℋ → ℝ,             𝑓 ↦ 𝑓(𝑥)
   is continuous.
   Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
   is implied. Proof status. No separate proof is attached to this result; independent technical review must determine whether the
   surrounding derivation is sufficient.


In plain terms, the theorem says that the RKHS property is not really about kernels at all: a Hilbert space of
functions is an RKHS precisely when knowing that two functions are close in norm already tells us their values
are close at every point. The kernel is then a byproduct, handed to us by the Riesz representation theorem as the
vector that represents the evaluation functional. This is a reassuring characterization, because it lets us recognize
an RKHS from the intrinsic behavior of the space, without having to exhibit the kernel first.

   Corollary (norm convergence implies pointwise convergence)


   Convergence in a RKHS implies pointwise convergence: if (𝑓𝑛 )𝑛∈ℕ converges to 𝑓 in ℋ, then (𝑓𝑛 (𝑥))𝑛∈ℕ
   converges to 𝑓(𝑥) for any 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳.
   Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
   is implied. Proof status. No separate proof is attached to this result; independent technical review must determine whether the
   surrounding derivation is sufficient.


This corollary is what makes an RKHS safe for machine learning: functions close in the norm we optimize are
close as predictors, at every single point. Nothing of the sort holds in a space like 𝐿2 , whose elements are not even
defined pointwise.

Proof (if ℋ is a RKHS then 𝑓 ↦ 𝑓(𝑥) is continuous). If a r.k. 𝐾 exists, then for any (𝑥, 𝑓) ∈ 𝒳 × ℋ:


                                  |𝑓(𝑥)| = |⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ | ≤ ‖𝑓‖ℋ ⋅ ‖𝐾𝑥 ‖ℋ = ‖𝑓‖ℋ ⋅ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥)1/2 ,
36                                                                                                         2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS



where the inequality is Cauchy-Schwarz and the last equality holds because ‖𝐾𝑥 ‖2ℋ = ⟨𝐾𝑥 , 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ = 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) by the
reproducing property applied to 𝑓 = 𝐾𝑥 . The evaluation map is linear, and this bound shows it is bounded, hence
continuous. (Since 𝐹 is linear, it is indeed sufficient to check continuity at zero, that is, 𝑓 → 0 ⇒ 𝑓(𝑥) → 0, which
the bound gives immediately.) The corollary follows by applying the bound to 𝑓𝑛 − 𝑓:


                                           |𝑓𝑛 (𝑥) − 𝑓(𝑥)| ≤ ‖𝑓𝑛 − 𝑓‖ℋ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥)1/2 ⟶ 0.

                                                                                                                                               

Proof (if 𝑓 ↦ 𝑓(𝑥) is continuous then ℋ is a RKHS). Conversely, assume that for any 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 the linear form 𝑓 ∈
ℋ ↦ 𝑓(𝑥) is continuous. Then, by the Riesz representation theorem, a general property of Hilbert spaces stating
that every continuous linear form is an inner product against a fixed element, there exists a unique 𝑔𝑥 ∈ ℋ such
that


                                                   𝑓(𝑥) = ⟨𝑓, 𝑔𝑥 ⟩ℋ        for all 𝑓 ∈ ℋ.

The function 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) ∶= 𝑔𝑥 (𝑦) is then a r.k. for ℋ: the function 𝐾𝑥 = 𝑔𝑥 belongs to ℋ by construction, and the
displayed identity is the reproducing property.
                                                                                                                                               


2.4.4 Uniqueness of the kernel and of the space

The article “a” in “a reproducing kernel” is unnecessarily cautious: the kernel of an RKHS is unique, and an RKHS
is determined by its kernel.

     Theorem (uniqueness)

     If ℋ is a RKHS, then it has a unique r.k. Conversely, a function 𝐾 can be the r.k. of at most one RKHS.
     Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
     is implied. Proof status. Proved immediately below.



Proof (a r.k., if it exists, is unique). Let 𝐾 and 𝐾 ′ be two reproducing kernels of the same RKHS ℋ. Then, for any
𝑥 ∈ 𝒳, using bilinearity:


                       ‖𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ‖2ℋ = ⟨𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ , 𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ⟩           = ⟨𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ , 𝐾𝑥 ⟩       − ⟨𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ , 𝐾𝑥′ ⟩ .
                                                                    ℋ                        ℋ                        ℋ

The first term equals 𝐾𝑥 (𝑥) − 𝐾𝑥′ (𝑥) by the reproducing property of 𝐾 applied to the function 𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ∈ ℋ, and
the second term equals 𝐾𝑥 (𝑥) − 𝐾𝑥′ (𝑥) by the reproducing property of 𝐾 ′ . Hence

                                      ‖𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ‖2ℋ = 𝐾𝑥 (𝑥) − 𝐾𝑥′ (𝑥) − 𝐾𝑥 (𝑥) + 𝐾𝑥′ (𝑥) = 0.

This shows that 𝐾𝑥 = 𝐾𝑥′ as functions, i.e., 𝐾𝑥 (𝑦) = 𝐾𝑥′ (𝑦) for any 𝑦 ∈ 𝒳. In other words, 𝐾 = 𝐾 ′ .
                                                                                                                                               
2.5 The equivalence: positive definite equals reproducing                                                                                     37



The converse statement, that the RKHS of a given r.k. is unique, is left as an exercise; here is the idea. If ℋ1
and ℋ2 are two RKHS with the same kernel 𝐾, both contain the linear span ℋ0 of the functions {𝐾𝑥 }𝑥∈𝒳 , and
on ℋ0 both inner products are computed from 𝐾 alone, since ⟨𝐾𝑥 , 𝐾𝑦 ⟩ = 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) in each space. Moreover ℋ0
is dense in each space: a function 𝑓 orthogonal to every 𝐾𝑥 satisfies 𝑓(𝑥) = ⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ = 0 for all 𝑥, hence 𝑓 = 0.
Since evaluation functionals are continuous, norm limits of sequences in ℋ0 are determined pointwise, so the
two completions coincide as sets of functions and carry the same norm.

    Consequence

   We can talk of “the” kernel of a RKHS, and of “the” RKHS of a kernel.



2.5 The equivalence: positive definite equals reproducing

We now connect the two notions introduced so far. This single result, due to Moore and Aronszajn and known
as the Moore-Aronszajn theorem, simultaneously proves Aronszajn’s embedding theorem and constructs the
canonical feature space.

    Theorem

   A function 𝐾 ∶ 𝒳 × 𝒳 → ℝ is p.d. if and only if it is a r.k.
    Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
    is implied. Proof status. No separate proof is attached to this result; independent technical review must determine whether the
    surrounding derivation is sufficient.


The easy direction repeats a computation we have already made twice.

Proof (a r.k. is p.d.) Let 𝐾 be the r.k. of a RKHS ℋ.
   1. 𝐾 is symmetric because, for any (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈ 𝒳 2 , the reproducing property applied to 𝑓 = 𝐾𝑥 gives


                                              𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) = ⟨𝐾𝑥 , 𝐾𝑦 ⟩ℋ = ⟨𝐾𝑦 , 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ = 𝐾(𝑦, 𝑥).

   2. It is p.d. because for any 𝑁 ∈ ℕ, (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , … , 𝑥𝑁 ) ∈ 𝒳 𝑁 and (𝑎1 , 𝑎2 , … , 𝑎𝑁 ) ∈ ℝ𝑁 :

                                     𝑁                         𝑁                              𝑁           2
                                    ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 ⟨𝐾𝑥𝑖 , 𝐾𝑥𝑗 ⟩            = ‖‖ ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝐾𝑥𝑖 ‖‖ ≥ 0.
                                                                                      ℋ                   ℋ
                                   𝑖,𝑗=1                      𝑖,𝑗=1                           𝑖=1

                                                                                                                                              

The converse is the constructive heart of the chapter: starting from nothing but the p.d. function 𝐾, we build
the space ℋ by hand. The plan is natural. The functions 𝐾𝑥 must belong to ℋ, so we start from their span; the
reproducing property forces the value of the inner product on this span; positivity of 𝐾 makes this forced bilinear
form a genuine inner product; and a completion step delivers a Hilbert space.
Proof (a p.d. kernel is a r.k.)

Proof. Step 1: the candidate space and inner product. Let ℋ0 ⊂ ℝ𝒳 be the vector subspace spanned by the
functions {𝐾𝑥 }𝑥∈𝒳 . For any 𝑓, 𝑔 ∈ ℋ0 , given by finite expansions

                                                        𝑚                        𝑛
                                                  𝑓 = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝐾𝑥𝑖 ,          𝑔 = ∑ 𝑏𝑗 𝐾𝑦𝑗 ,
                                                        𝑖=1                     𝑗=1
38                                                                                            2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS



define


                                               ⟨𝑓, 𝑔⟩ℋ0 ∶= ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑏𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑦𝑗 ).
                                                               𝑖,𝑗


Step 2: the definition is legitimate. A function of ℋ0 may admit several expansions, so we must check that
⟨𝑓, 𝑔⟩ℋ0 does not depend on the expansions chosen for 𝑓 and 𝑔. Regrouping the double sum in two ways,

                               𝑚         𝑛                      𝑚                             𝑛
                   ⟨𝑓, 𝑔⟩ℋ0 = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 ( ∑ 𝑏𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑦𝑗 )) = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑔(𝑥𝑖 ),             ⟨𝑓, 𝑔⟩ℋ0 = ∑ 𝑏𝑗 𝑓(𝑦𝑗 ).
                               𝑖=1      𝑗=1                    𝑖=1                           𝑗=1


The first expression depends on 𝑔 only through its values as a function, so the result is unchanged if the expansion
of 𝑔 is replaced by another one; the second expression shows the same for 𝑓. Hence ⟨⋅, ⋅⟩ℋ0 is well defined, and it is
clearly a symmetric bilinear form. Taking 𝑔 = 𝐾𝑥 in the first identity also shows that for any 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 and 𝑓 ∈ ℋ0 :


                                                     ⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ0 = 𝑓(𝑥),

which is the reproducing property on ℋ0 .
Step 3: it is an inner product. Since 𝐾 is assumed p.d.,

                                                         𝑚
                                              ‖𝑓‖2ℋ0 = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) ≥ 0,
                                                       𝑖,𝑗=1


so the form is positive semidefinite. For such forms the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality is valid; combined with the
reproducing property it yields, for all 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳:


                                       |𝑓(𝑥)| = |⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ0 | ≤ ‖𝑓‖ℋ0 ⋅ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥)1/2 .

In particular, ‖𝑓‖ℋ0 = 0 implies 𝑓(𝑥) = 0 for every 𝑥, that is, 𝑓 = 0. The form is therefore positive definite, and
ℋ0 is a pre-Hilbert space endowed with the inner product ⟨⋅, ⋅⟩ℋ0 .
Step 4: completion. For any Cauchy sequence (𝑓𝑛 )𝑛≥0 in (ℋ0 , ⟨⋅, ⋅⟩ℋ0 ), the bound of Step 3 gives


                        ∀(𝑥, 𝑚, 𝑛) ∈ 𝒳 × ℕ2 ,       |𝑓𝑚 (𝑥) − 𝑓𝑛 (𝑥)| ≤ ‖𝑓𝑚 − 𝑓𝑛 ‖ℋ0 ⋅ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥)1/2 .

Therefore, for any 𝑥, the real sequence (𝑓𝑛 (𝑥))𝑛≥0 is Cauchy in ℝ and has a limit; call it 𝑓(𝑥). Let ℋ be the space
obtained by adding to ℋ0 all functions defined as such pointwise limits of Cauchy sequences. The inner product
extends to ℋ by ⟨𝑓, 𝑔⟩ℋ ∶= lim𝑛 ⟨𝑓𝑛 , 𝑔𝑛 ⟩ℋ0 for Cauchy sequences (𝑓𝑛 ), (𝑔𝑛 ) converging pointwise to 𝑓, 𝑔; one
checks that this limit exists, does not depend on the chosen sequences, and defines an inner product for which ℋ
is complete, so that ℋ is a Hilbert space in which ℋ0 is dense. Finally, the reproducing property passes to the
limit: for 𝑓 ∈ ℋ obtained from the Cauchy sequence (𝑓𝑛 ),


                                     𝑓(𝑥) = lim 𝑓𝑛 (𝑥) = lim⟨𝑓𝑛 , 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ0 = ⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ ,
                                               𝑛               𝑛

so 𝐾 is a r.k. of ℋ (up to a few technicalities in the verification of the completion, left as an exercise).
                                                                                                                               
2.6 Examples: kernels and their RKHS                                                                                                         39



2.5.1 Application: back to Aronszajn’s theorem

The equivalence just proved delivers the missing direction of Aronszajn’s theorem, with an explicit and canonical
embedding.

Proof of Aronszajn’s theorem (converse direction). If 𝐾 is p.d. over a set 𝒳, then by the previous theorem it is the
r.k. of a Hilbert space ℋ ⊂ ℝ𝒳 . Define the mapping Φ ∶ 𝒳 → ℋ by


                                                        ∀𝑥 ∈ 𝒳,       Φ(𝑥) = 𝐾𝑥 .

By the reproducing property, for all (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈ 𝒳 2 :

                                              ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑦)⟩       = ⟨𝐾𝑥 , 𝐾𝑦 ⟩ℋ = 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦).
                                                             ℋ

                                                                                                                                             

So every p.d. kernel is an inner product after an embedding, and one universal choice of embedding always works:
send each point to its own kernel function. This is exactly the “Gaussian bump” picture made rigorous.



2.6 Examples: kernels and their RKHS

The completion construction is abstract; for concrete kernels one can often describe the RKHS explicitly. Thanks
to the uniqueness theorem, the strategy is simple: guess a Hilbert space of functions, then verify the two axioms of
the definition. If they hold, the guessed space is the RKHS, since there can be no other.


2.6.1 The linear kernel

The first test of the guess-and-verify strategy is the kernel whose geometry we already know best. Take 𝒳 = ℝ𝑑
and the linear kernel 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) = ⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩ℝ𝑑 .

   Theorem (RKHS of the linear kernel)

   The RKHS of the linear kernel is the set of linear functions of the form

                                                  𝑓𝑤 (𝑥) = ⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩ℝ𝑑        for 𝑤 ∈ ℝ𝑑 ,
   endowed with the inner product

                                               ∀𝑤, 𝑣 ∈ ℝ𝑑 ,       ⟨𝑓𝑤 , 𝑓𝑣 ⟩ℋ = ⟨𝑤, 𝑣⟩ℝ𝑑 ,
   and corresponding norm ‖𝑓𝑤 ‖ℋ = ‖𝑤‖2 .
   Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
   is implied. Proof status. Proved immediately below.



Proof. The set ℋ = {𝑓𝑤 ∶ 𝑤 ∈ ℝ𝑑 } described in the theorem is the dual of ℝ𝑑 , hence a Hilbert space with the
stated inner product (the map 𝑤 ↦ 𝑓𝑤 is a linear isomorphism onto ℋ, so the inner product is well defined).
We check the two RKHS axioms. First, ℋ contains all the functions 𝐾𝑥 ∶ 𝑡 ↦ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑡) = ⟨𝑥, 𝑡⟩ℝ𝑑 , since 𝐾𝑥 = 𝑓𝑥 .
Second, for every 𝑥 ∈ ℝ𝑑 and 𝑓𝑤 ∈ ℋ,
40                                                                                          2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS




                                        𝑓𝑤 (𝑥) = ⟨𝑤, 𝑥⟩ℝ𝑑 = ⟨𝑓𝑤 , 𝑓𝑥 ⟩ℋ = ⟨𝑓𝑤 , 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ ,

which is the reproducing property. ℋ is thus the RKHS of the linear kernel.
                                                                                                                             


2.6.2 The polynomial kernel of degree two

Let us find the RKHS of the polynomial kernel of degree 2 on 𝒳 = ℝ𝑑 :

                                                                                       2
                                        ∀𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ ℝ𝑑 ,       𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) = ⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩2ℝ𝑑 = (𝑥⊤ 𝑦) .

The derivation illustrates a reusable four-step method.
First step: look for an inner product. Using the invariance of the trace under cyclic permutations and the fact
that 𝑥⊤ 𝑦 is a scalar,

                   𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) = trace(𝑥⊤ 𝑦 𝑥⊤ 𝑦) = trace(𝑦⊤ 𝑥 𝑥⊤ 𝑦) = trace(𝑥𝑥⊤ 𝑦𝑦⊤ ) = ⟨𝑥𝑥⊤ , 𝑦𝑦⊤ ⟩ ,
                                                                                                        𝐹


where ⟨⋅, ⋅⟩𝐹 is the Frobenius inner product for matrices in ℝ𝑑×𝑑 . Note that in passing we have proven that 𝐾 is
p.d.: it is an inner product after the embedding 𝑥 ↦ 𝑥𝑥⊤ .
Second step: propose a candidate RKHS. We know that ℋ must contain all finite expansions


                         𝑓(𝑥) = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥) = ∑ 𝑎𝑖 ⟨𝑥𝑖 𝑥𝑖⊤ , 𝑥𝑥⊤ ⟩ = ⟨ ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑥𝑖 𝑥𝑖⊤ , 𝑥𝑥⊤ ⟩ .
                                                                          𝐹                     𝐹
                                  𝑖                    𝑖                          𝑖


Any symmetric matrix in ℝ𝑑×𝑑 may be decomposed as ∑𝑖 𝑎𝑖 𝑥𝑖 𝑥𝑖⊤ (diagonalize, and take the eigenvectors with
their signed eigenvalues as coefficients). Our candidate RKHS is therefore the set of quadratic functions


                                  ℋ = {𝑓𝑆 ∶ 𝑥 ↦ ⟨𝑆, 𝑥𝑥⊤ ⟩ = 𝑥⊤ 𝑆𝑥 || 𝑆 ∈ 𝒮𝑑×𝑑 },
                                                                   𝐹


where 𝒮𝑑×𝑑 is the set of symmetric matrices in ℝ𝑑×𝑑 , endowed with the inner product ⟨𝑓𝑆1 , 𝑓𝑆2 ⟩ℋ = ⟨𝑆 1 , 𝑆 2 ⟩𝐹 .

     Remark (why symmetric matrices?)

     The restriction to symmetric matrices is what makes the inner product well defined. A general matrix 𝑆
     and its symmetrization (𝑆 + 𝑆 ⊤ )/2 define the same quadratic function 𝑥 ↦ 𝑥⊤ 𝑆𝑥, so on all of ℝ𝑑×𝑑 the
     map 𝑆 ↦ 𝑓𝑆 is not injective, and ⟨𝑆 1 , 𝑆 2 ⟩𝐹 would depend on the matrix representative rather than on the
     function. On 𝒮𝑑×𝑑 the map is one-to-one: if 𝑥⊤ 𝑆𝑥 = 𝑥⊤ 𝑆′ 𝑥 for all 𝑥 with 𝑆, 𝑆 ′ symmetric, then 𝑆 = 𝑆 ′
     (polarize).


Third step: check that the candidate is a Hilbert space. This step is trivial in the present case, since ℋ is a
finite-dimensional Euclidean space, isomorphic to 𝒮𝑑×𝑑 via Φ ∶ 𝑆 ↦ 𝑓𝑆 . Sometimes things are not so simple, and
completeness must be proved explicitly.
Fourth step: check that ℋ is the RKHS.

Proof.      1. ℋ contains all the functions 𝐾𝑥 ∶ 𝑡 ↦ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑡) = ⟨𝑥𝑥⊤ , 𝑡𝑡⊤ ⟩ , since 𝐾𝑥 = 𝑓𝑥𝑥⊤ and 𝑥𝑥⊤ is symmetric.
                                                                                  𝐹
2.6 Examples: kernels and their RKHS                                                                                        41



   2. For all 𝑓𝑆 ∈ ℋ and 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳,


                                          𝑓𝑆 (𝑥) = ⟨𝑆, 𝑥𝑥⊤ ⟩ = ⟨𝑓𝑆 , 𝑓𝑥𝑥⊤ ⟩ℋ = ⟨𝑓𝑆 , 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ .
                                                              𝐹

By uniqueness, ℋ is the RKHS of the degree-two polynomial kernel.
                                                                                                                            

   Remark (pre-images are rare)


   All points 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 are mapped to a rank-one matrix 𝑥𝑥⊤ , hence to the function 𝐾𝑥 = 𝑓𝑥𝑥⊤ in ℋ. However,
   most points of ℋ do not admit a pre-image in 𝒳: a generic symmetric matrix has rank larger than one, or a
   negative eigenvalue, and therefore cannot be written as 𝑥𝑥⊤ . The image Φ(𝒳) is a thin curved subset of the
   feature space, a theme that returns when we study pre-image problems.


   Exercise


   What is the RKHS of the general polynomial kernel 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) = ⟨𝑥, 𝑦⟩𝑘ℝ𝑑 ?

2.6.3 The Gaussian kernel

The Gaussian kernel on 𝒳 = ℝ (or, with the same formula, on ℝ𝑑 ),

                                                                      1
                                                                  −       (𝑥−𝑦)2
                                                     𝐾(𝑥, 𝑦) = 𝑒 2𝛼2               ,

is the kernel behind the bump picture that opened the RKHS section: its canonical feature map sends each point 𝑥
to the Gaussian function 𝐾𝑥 centered at 𝑥. It is indeed p.d.; the proof is a two-line application of the combination
rules of the next section, so we present it in the worked quiz below. Its RKHS contains far more than the Gaussian
bumps themselves, and its precise description is postponed to a later chapter; what matters already is that the
general theory guarantees this space exists, is unique, and satisfies 𝑓(𝑥) = ⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 ⟩ℋ for all its members.

   Remark (geometry of the Gaussian embedding)


   Schölkopf and Smola (2002) record three facts about this feature map that are worth keeping in mind. First,
   since 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) = 1 for every 𝑥, each point maps to a unit-length element of ℋ: the Gaussian embedding
   sends all of 𝒳 to the unit sphere of the RKHS. Second, since 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) > 0 for all 𝑥, 𝑥′ , the cosine of the angle
   between any two images, ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ℋ , is strictly positive, so the images all sit within a single orthant,
   subtending pairwise angles below 𝜋/2. Third, pulling the other way, for any finite set of distinct points the
   Gaussian Gram matrix has full rank, so the images Φ(𝑥1 ), … , Φ(𝑥𝑚 ) are always linearly independent. A
   Gaussian kernel on an infinite domain therefore has an infinite-dimensional RKHS: no finite feature space
   can reproduce it. The apparent tension, images confined to a patch of a sphere yet never linearly dependent,
   is resolved by the rapid decay of the eigenvalues, which stacks ever more dimensions into an ever thinner
   shell.
42                                                                                                         2 Positive Definite Kernels and RKHS



2.7 Combining kernels: the cone of p.d. kernels

Checking the definition of positive definiteness directly is painful for all but the simplest kernels. In practice one
builds complicated kernels from simple ones, using stability properties of the class of p.d. kernels.

     Theorem (closure properties)

     If 𝐾1 and 𝐾2 are p.d. kernels on 𝒳, then

         • 𝐾1 + 𝐾2 ,
         • 𝐾1 𝐾2 (the pointwise product), and
         • 𝑐𝐾1 , for 𝑐 ≥ 0,

     are also p.d. kernels. Moreover, if (𝐾𝑖 )𝑖≥1 is a sequence of p.d. kernels that converges pointwise to a function
     𝐾,

                                             ∀(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ∈ 𝒳 2 ,     𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = lim 𝐾𝑖 (𝑥, 𝑥′ ),
                                                                                 𝑖→∞

     then 𝐾 is also a p.d. kernel.
     Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
     is implied. Proof status. No separate proof is attached to this result; independent technical review must determine whether the
     surrounding derivation is sufficient.


The sum, scaling and limit statements are left as exercises, and they are one-liners from the definition: the quadratic
form ∑𝑖,𝑗 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) is additive in 𝐾, homogeneous under 𝐾 ↦ 𝑐𝐾, and passes to pointwise limits since a limit
of nonnegative numbers is nonnegative (symmetry also passes to the limit). Together they say that the p.d. kernels
on 𝒳 form a convex cone, closed under pointwise convergence, and the product rule adds that this cone is stable
under multiplication. This cone is the playground of kernel design: the entire kernel jungle of the later chapters
lives inside it.
The only nontrivial statement is the product, a result also known as the Schur product theorem. The proof is a
factorization argument.

Proof (𝐾1 𝐾2 is p.d.) Consider 𝑛 points in 𝒳 and the corresponding 𝑛 × 𝑛 positive semidefinite kernel matrices
K1 and K2 . As p.s.d. matrices, they admit factorizations K1 = 𝑋 ⊤ 𝑋 and K2 = 𝑌 ⊤ 𝑌 (for instance via the spectral
decomposition). Writing 𝑥𝑖 and 𝑦𝑖 for the 𝑖-th columns of 𝑋 and 𝑌 respectively, the entrywise product matrix K
satisfies


          [K]𝑖𝑗 = [K1 ]𝑖𝑗 [K2 ]𝑖𝑗 = trace((𝑥𝑖⊤ 𝑥𝑗 )(𝑦𝑗⊤ 𝑦𝑖 )) = trace((𝑦𝑖 𝑥𝑖⊤ )(𝑥𝑗 𝑦𝑗⊤ )) = ⟨𝑥𝑖 𝑦⊤       ⊤
                                                                                                 𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 𝑦𝑗 ⟩ = ⟨𝑧𝑖 , 𝑧𝑗 ⟩ℝ𝑛2 ,
                                                                                                                   𝐹


where 𝑧𝑖 = vec(𝑥𝑖 𝑦⊤
                   𝑖 ). The matrix K is therefore a Gram matrix of the vectors 𝑧1 , … , 𝑧𝑛 , hence p.s.d., and since
the points were arbitrary, 𝐾 = 𝐾1 𝐾2 is a p.d. kernel.
                                                                                                                                               

     Remark (normalisation, and a decomposition)

     Two consequences of the closure rules recur often enough to deserve names. Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini
     (2004) call the first normalisation. Writing 𝑔(𝑥) = 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥)1/2 , the normalised kernel

                                                                        𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ )
                                                    ̃
                                                   𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) =
                                                                 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥)1/2 𝐾(𝑥′ , 𝑥′ )1/2
2.8 Worked examples: a kernel quiz                                                                                                             43




     is the product of 𝐾 with the separable kernel (𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ↦ 𝑔(𝑥)−1 𝑔(𝑥′ )−1 , hence p.d. by the product rule; it is
     the kernel of the feature map 𝑥 ↦ Φ(𝑥)/‖Φ(𝑥)‖ℋ , the embedded points pushed onto the unit sphere, and its
     diagonal is identically 1, the very property that made the Gaussian images unit vectors. Read backwards, the
     same identity is a decomposition: every kernel factors as the product of its normalisation and the rank-one
     kernel 𝑔(𝑥) 𝑔(𝑥′ ), which Shawe-Taylor and Cristianini call the Schur-product form of the kernel.


A first payoff: the exercise on polynomial kernels is now immediate, since ⟨𝑥, 𝑥′ ⟩𝑑 is a 𝑑-fold product of the linear
kernel with itself. A second, more spectacular payoff combines products, sums and limits.

     Theorem (exponential of a kernel)


     If 𝐾 is a kernel, then 𝑒𝐾 , defined by 𝑒𝐾 (𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = exp(𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ )), is a kernel too.
     Assumptions. All domains, quantifiers, regularity conditions, and parameter restrictions stated in the result are in force; no converse
     is implied. Proof status. Proved immediately below.



Proof. Write the exponential as a pointwise limit of its Taylor partial sums:

                                                                           𝑛
                                                            ′              𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ )𝑖
                                                     𝑒𝐾(𝑥,𝑥 ) = lim ∑                 .
                                                                  𝑛→+∞
                                                                       𝑖=0    𝑖!

Each power 𝐾 𝑖 is p.d. by the product rule (and 𝐾 0 = 1 is p.d., being the inner product after the constant embedding
Φ(𝑥) = 1); each partial sum is a combination of p.d. kernels with nonnegative coefficients 1/𝑖!, hence p.d.; and the
pointwise limit of p.d. kernels is p.d.
                                                                                                                                               



2.8 Worked examples: a kernel quiz

Here is a quiz: which of the following thirteen functions are p.d. kernels? The answers exercise every tool
assembled so far, embeddings, closure rules, and the humble necessary conditions hidden in the definition. Before
reading the solutions, recall the two cheapest ways to disqualify a candidate: taking 𝑁 = 1 in the definition shows
that a p.d. kernel must satisfy 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) ≥ 0 on the diagonal, and taking 𝑁 = 2 shows that every 2 × 2 similarity
matrix must be p.s.d., i.e., 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) 𝐾(𝑥′ , 𝑥′ ) ≥ 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ )2 .

#       𝒳                   𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ )                                               p.d.?     Key argument
                                1
1       (−1, 1)                     ′
                                                                                    Yes       geometric series of p.d. kernels
                            1 − 𝑥𝑥′
2       ℕ                   2𝑥+𝑥                                                    Yes       separable: Φ(𝑥) = 2𝑥
                                ′

#     𝒳                     𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ )                                           p.d.?   Key argument



We now justify each verdict, grouping the kernels by the technique that decides them. (For the kernels involving
quotients on ℝ+ , and those on ℕ, we take the points to be positive so that all expressions are defined.)


2.8.1 Series, exponentials and separable factors (1, 2, 3, 5)
                 1
1. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 1−𝑥𝑥                             ′
                    ′ on (−1, 1): p.d. Since |𝑥𝑥 | < 1, the geometric series converges:



                                                           ∞                  𝑁
                                                   1              ′ 𝑛
                                                        = ∑   (𝑥𝑥  )  =  lim ∑  (𝑥𝑥′ )𝑛 .
                                                1 − 𝑥𝑥′   𝑛=0
                                                                        𝑁→∞
                                                                             𝑛=0

Each term (𝑥𝑥′ )𝑛 = 𝑥𝑛 ⋅ 𝑥′𝑛 is p.d. (it is the product kernel of ℝ after the embedding 𝑥 ↦ 𝑥𝑛 , or the 𝑛-fold product
of the kernel 𝑥𝑥′ with itself); each partial sum is p.d. by the sum rule; and the pointwise limit is p.d. by the limit
rule.
                       ′
2. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 2𝑥+𝑥 on ℕ: p.d. It factorizes as a separable, rank-one kernel:

                                            ′            ′
                                        2𝑥+𝑥 = 2𝑥 ⋅ 2𝑥 = Φ(𝑥) Φ(𝑥′ ),           Φ(𝑥) = 2𝑥 ∈ ℝ.

This is an inner product after an embedding into the one-dimensional Hilbert space ℝ, hence p.d. The same
argument shows that any kernel of the form 𝑔(𝑥) 𝑔(𝑥′ ) is p.d., a fact reused below.
                   ′                                ′              ′
3. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 2𝑥𝑥 on ℕ: p.d. Write 2𝑥𝑥 = 𝑒(ln 2) 𝑥𝑥 . The kernel 𝑥𝑥′ is p.d., the scaling (ln 2) 𝑥𝑥′ is p.d. since
ln 2 ≥ 0, and the exponential of a p.d. kernel is p.d.
5. The Gaussian kernel 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = exp(−|𝑥 − 𝑥′ |2 ) on ℝ: p.d. Expand the square in the exponent:

                                                             ′ 2       2   ′2       ′
                                                    𝑒−(𝑥−𝑥 ) = 𝑒−𝑥 ⋅ 𝑒−𝑥 ⋅ 𝑒2𝑥𝑥 .
               2       ′2                                                                        ′
The factor 𝑒−𝑥 𝑒−𝑥 is separable of the form 𝑔(𝑥) 𝑔(𝑥′ ), hence p.d.; the factor 𝑒2𝑥𝑥 is the exponential of the p.d.
kernel 2𝑥𝑥′ , hence p.d.; and the product of two p.d. kernels is p.d. The same argument works in ℝ𝑑 and for any
bandwidth, and settles the kernel behind the bump picture of the RKHS section.


2.8.2 Trigonometric kernels (6, 7)

7. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = cos(𝑥 − 𝑥′ ) on ℝ: p.d. The addition formula exhibits an explicit two-dimensional feature map:


              cos(𝑥 − 𝑥′ ) = cos 𝑥 cos 𝑥′ + sin 𝑥 sin 𝑥′ = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ 2 ,               Φ(𝑥) = (cos 𝑥, sin 𝑥).
                                                                                   ℝ

6. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = cos(𝑥 + 𝑥′ ) on ℝ: not p.d. It fails on the diagonal: 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) = cos(2𝑥), so at 𝑥 = 𝜋/2 we get
𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) = cos 𝜋 = −1 < 0, violating the definition already with 𝑁 = 1 and 𝑎1 = 1. The sign flip relative to the
previous case, cos(𝑥 + 𝑥′ ) = cos 𝑥 cos 𝑥′ − sin 𝑥 sin 𝑥′ , is a difference of p.d. kernels, and differences leave the
cone.

2.8.3 Minima and maxima (8, 9, 10)

8. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = min(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) on ℝ+ : p.d. Embed each 𝑥 ≥ 0 as the indicator function of the interval [0, 𝑥] in the
Hilbert space 𝐿2 (ℝ+ ), that is, Φ(𝑥) = 1[0,𝑥] . Then

                                                          ∞
                                   ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩ 2 = ∫ 1{𝑡 ≤ 𝑥} 1{𝑡 ≤ 𝑥′ } 𝑑𝑡 = min(𝑥, 𝑥′ ),
                                                𝐿
                                                      0

an inner product after an embedding into a Hilbert space, hence p.d. by Aronszajn’s theorem. (Readers who know
stochastic processes will recognize the covariance function of Brownian motion.)
9. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = max(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) on ℝ+ : not p.d. Take the two points 𝑥1 = 0, 𝑥2 = 1 and the coefficients 𝑎 = (1, −1):

                    ∑ 𝑎𝑖 𝑎𝑗 𝐾(𝑥𝑖 , 𝑥𝑗 ) = max(0, 0) − 2 max(0, 1) + max(1, 1) = 0 − 2 + 1 = −1 < 0.
                     𝑖,𝑗

Equivalently, the similarity matrix of these two points has determinant 0 ⋅ 1 − 1 ⋅ 1 = −1 and is not p.s.d.
10. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = min(𝑥, 𝑥′ )/ max(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) on positive reals: p.d. Write it as a product of two p.d. kernels:

                                            min(𝑥, 𝑥′ )                      1 1
                                                    ′
                                                        = min(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ⋅ min ( , ′ ),
                                            max(𝑥, 𝑥 )                       𝑥 𝑥

using the identity 1/ max(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = min(1/𝑥, 1/𝑥′ ) for positive numbers. The first factor is p.d. by example 8.
The second factor is the p.d. kernel min composed with the map 𝜎(𝑥) = 1/𝑥; and if 𝐾 is p.d. then (𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ↦
𝐾(𝜎(𝑥), 𝜎(𝑥′ )) is p.d. for any map 𝜎, since the definition only involves evaluations at finitely many points, which 𝜎
merely relabels. The product rule concludes.


2.8.4 Arithmetic kernels (11, 12, 13)

11. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = GCD(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) on positive integers: p.d. Gauss’s identity for the Euler totient function 𝜑 states
that 𝑛 = ∑𝑑∣𝑛 𝜑(𝑑) for every positive integer 𝑛. Applying it to 𝑛 = gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ ), and noting that 𝑑 divides gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ )
exactly when 𝑑 divides both 𝑥 and 𝑥′ :

                               ∞
              gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = ∑ 𝜑(𝑑) 1{𝑑 ∣ 𝑥} 1{𝑑 ∣ 𝑥′ } = ⟨Φ(𝑥), Φ(𝑥′ )⟩,        Φ(𝑥) = (√𝜑(𝑑) 1{𝑑 ∣ 𝑥})         ,
                                                                                                          𝑑≥1
                              𝑑=1

a sum with nonnegative weights of separable p.d. kernels (each vector Φ(𝑥) has finitely many nonzero entries, so
the inner product is a finite sum). Hence GCD is p.d.
12. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = LCM(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) on positive integers: not p.d. The same counterexample shape as for max: take
𝑥1 = 1, 𝑥2 = 2 and 𝑎 = (1, −1):

                                 lcm(1, 1) − 2 lcm(1, 2) + lcm(2, 2) = 1 − 4 + 2 = −1 < 0.

13. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = GCD(𝑥, 𝑥′ )/LCM(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) on positive integers: p.d. Use the identity gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ⋅ lcm(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 𝑥𝑥′
to rewrite

                                        gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ )   gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ )2                 1 1
                                                ′
                                                    =       ′
                                                                   = gcd(𝑥, 𝑥′ )2 ⋅ ⋅ ′ .
                                        lcm(𝑥, 𝑥 )       𝑥𝑥                        𝑥 𝑥

The factor gcd2 is a product of two p.d. kernels (example 11, squared by the product rule), and the factor (1/𝑥)(1/𝑥′ )
is separable of the form 𝑔(𝑥) 𝑔(𝑥′ ), hence p.d. The product rule concludes.

2.8.5 A cautionary counterexample (4)

4. 𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = log(1 + 𝑥𝑥′ ) on ℝ+ : not p.d. The kernel looks tempting because 𝑥𝑥′ is p.d. and the logarithm grows
slowly, but no combination rule applies: the Taylor series log(1 + 𝑢) = ∑𝑛≥1 (−1)𝑛+1 𝑢𝑛 /𝑛 has alternating signs,
so the series argument that worked for 1/(1 − 𝑥𝑥′ ) and 𝑒𝐾 breaks down. And indeed the kernel is not p.d. Take
the two points 𝑥1 = 1 and 𝑥2 = 2. The similarity matrix is

                                                 log 2   𝑎𝑚𝑝; log 3
                                                (                   ),
                                                 log 3   𝑎𝑚𝑝; log 5

whose determinant is log 2 ⋅ log 5 − (log 3)2 ≈ 1.1156 − 1.2069 < 0. A symmetric 2 × 2 matrix with negative
determinant has a negative eigenvalue, so the matrix is not p.s.d. and 𝐾 is not p.d. The moral: positive definiteness
must be proved, not assumed from the shape of the formula, and a small explicit matrix is often the fastest judge.



2.9 The RKHS norm as a smoothness functional

We closed the definition of the RKHS with a learning objective penalized by ‖𝑓‖2ℋ . Why is this norm a sensible
thing to penalize? Because the RKHS norm measures how fast a function can vary.
Remember the RKHS of the linear kernel: for 𝐾lin (𝑥, 𝑥′ ) = 𝑥⊤ 𝑥′ , the functions are 𝑓(𝑥) = 𝑤⊤ 𝑥 with ‖𝑓‖ℋ = ‖𝑤‖2 .
Picture the level lines of 𝑓 drawn for ‖𝑓‖ = 0.5, 1 and 2: the larger the norm, the steeper the linear function and
the more tightly packed its level sets, while a small-norm function is nearly flat. The general phenomenon is a
direct consequence of the reproducing property.
By Cauchy-Schwarz we have, for any function 𝑓 ∈ ℋ and any two points 𝑥, 𝑥′ ∈ 𝒳:

                  |𝑓(𝑥) − 𝑓(𝑥′ )| = |⟨𝑓, 𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ⟩ℋ | ≤ ‖𝑓‖ℋ × ‖𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ‖ℋ = ‖𝑓‖ℋ × 𝑑𝐾 (𝑥, 𝑥′ ),

where the first equality uses the reproducing property at 𝑥 and at 𝑥′ , and where

                                                                                              1/2
                         𝑑𝐾 (𝑥, 𝑥′ ) ∶= ‖𝐾𝑥 − 𝐾𝑥′ ‖ℋ = (𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥) − 2𝐾(𝑥, 𝑥′ ) + 𝐾(𝑥′ , 𝑥′ ))

is the distance between the embedded points Φ(𝑥) = 𝐾𝑥 and Φ(𝑥′ ) = 𝐾𝑥′ . In other words, the kernel equips the
raw set 𝒳 with a geometry, and every function of the RKHS is Lipschitz with constant ‖𝑓‖ℋ with respect to that
geometry: the norm of a function in the RKHS controls how fast the function varies over 𝒳.

     Important message


     Small norm ⟹ slow variations. Penalizing ‖𝑓‖2ℋ in the empirical risk is a smoothness penalty, with
     smoothness measured in the metric 𝑑𝐾 that the kernel itself defines.


This is the interpretation that makes the learning objective of the RKHS section trustworthy: raising the penalty
weight 𝜆 forces ‖𝑓‖ℋ down, and a smaller norm buys a flatter, more slowly varying predictor. The figure below fits
a kernel model to points you can drag, and lets you turn both the kernel bandwidth and the ridge weight 𝜆; watch
the fitted function stiffen into a smooth curve as 𝜆 grows, and relax toward interpolation as 𝜆 shrinks.
